Conditional logic as a short-circuit logic
Jan A. Bergstra & Alban Ponse

Abstract
Three-valued conditional logic (CL) is defined by Guzmán and Squier (1990), and based on McCarthy’s noncommutative connectives, axiomatises a short-circuit logic (SCL) that defines more identities than three-valued MSCL (Memorising SCL, which also has a two-valued variant). This follows from the fact that the definable connective that prescribes full left-sequential conjunction is commutative in CL. We show that in CL, the full left-sequential connectives and negation define Bochvar’s three-valued strict logic. We observe that CL also has a two-valued variant of which the full left-sequential connectives and negation define a commutative logic that is weaker than propositional logic because the absorption laws do not hold. Next, we show that the original, equational axiomatisation of CL is not independent and give several alternative, independent axiomatisations.



Keywords & phrases:

Conditional logic, short-circuit evaluation, short-circuit logic,

left-sequential connectives, Bochvar’s logic

Conditional logic (CL) is defined by
Guzmán and Squier in the paper
The algebra of conditional logic

[
11
]
, where
the conjunction adapts McCarthy’s
conjunction to the domain of the three truth values
true
,
false
,
undefined
with constants

{\sf T}
,
{\sf F}
,
{\sf U}
, respectively.
In CL, the 3-element algebra
C=\{{\sf T},{\sf F},{\sf U}\}
is studied with the operations

x\mapsto x^{\prime}:C\to C
and
(x,y)\mapsto x\wedge y,x\vee y:C^{2}\to C
given by the tables

\begin{array}[t]{l|l}&{}^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr\\[-8.53581pt]
{\sf T}&{\sf F}\\
{\sf F}&{\sf T}\\
{\sf U}&{\sf U}\end{array}\hskip 56.9055pt\begin{array}[t]{l|lll}\wedge&{\sf T}&{\sf F}&{\sf U}\\
\hline\cr\\[-8.53581pt]
{\sf T}&{\sf T}&{\sf F}&{\sf U}\\
{\sf F}&{\sf F}&{\sf F}&{\sf F}\\
{\sf U}&{\sf U}&{\sf U}&{\sf U}\end{array}\hskip 56.9055pt\begin{array}[t]{l|lll}\vee&{\sf T}&{\sf F}&{\sf U}\\
\hline\cr\\[-8.53581pt]
{\sf T}&{\sf T}&{\sf T}&{\sf T}\\
{\sf F}&{\sf T}&{\sf F}&{\sf U}\\
{\sf U}&{\sf U}&{\sf U}&{\sf U}\end{array}

A
C
-algebra
is defined as an algebra with the operations
′
,
\wedge
and
\vee
.
This set-up does not require that the constants are defined in a
C
-algebra, and thus includes
four basic cases:

(i)

no distinguished elements,

(ii)

{\sf U}
distinguished,

(iii)

{\sf T}
and
{\sf F}
distinguished, and

(iv)

all elements of
C
distinguished.

In
[
11
]
it is shown that the laws in Table
1
are complete
for the variety of
C
-algebras.
Laws (
1.1a
) and (
1.1b
) imply that this variety satisfies a
duality principle identical to the one in ordinary Boolean algebra.
For the case that the constants for these three truth values are distinguished, the
following three axioms are added:

{\sf U}^{\prime}={\sf U},\quad{\sf T}\wedge x=x,\quad{\sf T}^{\prime}={\sf F}.

We end this brief introduction to CL with a quote from
[
11
]
about the name “conditional logic”
and “short-circuit evaluation” :

“For example, let
B=\{T,F\}
with ordinary negation (
′
),
conjunction (
\wedge
) and disjunction (
\vee
).
[…]

Up to anti-isomorphism, there is a unique regular extension of
B
to a 3-valued logic with
non-commutative
\wedge
and
\vee
which satisfies deMorgan’s laws
x^{\prime\prime}=x
and
(x\wedge y)^{\prime}=x^{\prime}\vee y^{\prime}
.
Following
[
10
]
, we call this algebra “conditional logic.” Conditional logic was first studied by
McCarthy
[
14
]
and
[
15
]
. It is the logic of choice in several programming languages (C, Prolog,
Lisp, …) in which the idea of “short circuit evaluation” is implemented.
It leads to faster evaluation of the logical expression, since evaluation
stops as soon as an answer can be obtained.”
[Reference numbering has been adjusted.]

We first explain why we view short-circuit evaluation (SCE) as the basic evaluation strategy
in any setting that
prescribes
sequential evaluation of
\wedge
and
\vee
.
Following
[
1
]
, we write

x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}y

for the conjunction of
x
and
y
that prescribes
SCE, where the small circle indicates that the left argument must be evaluated first, and
use the name
short-circuit conjunction
.
We write
~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}

for
short-circuit disjunction
, which as above is defined dually:

x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}y=\neg(\neg x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg y).

From now on, we will mostly use
~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}
and
~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}
and
\neg
when writing about
CL
.
We write

\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}\quad\text{ and }\quad\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{3}

respectively for the case where constants
{\sf T}
and
{\sf F}
are
distinguished and the two defining axioms
\neg{\sf T}={\sf F}
and
{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x=x
are added to those
of
CL
, and the case where
{\sf U}
is also distinguished and the axiom
\neg{\sf U}={\sf U}
is added.
If no constants are distinguished, we simple write
CL
.

Another well-known sequential evaluation strategy is
full left-sequential evaluation
,
where both arguments of a conjunction
and a disjunction are always evaluated. The connective
full left-sequential conjunction
, denoted by

x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y
,
prescribes full left-sequential evaluation and can be defined in terms of SCE:

x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y=(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F}))~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}y.

Thus, if
x
evaluates to
true
,
y
is evaluated and determines the evaluation value, and if

x
evaluates to
false
,
y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F}
is evaluated, which determines that

x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y
has the value
false
. The connective
full left-sequential disjunction
,
denoted by
x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y
, is defined dually:
x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y=\neg(\neg x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg y)
.

Short-circuit evaluation admits several semantics (valuation congruences), which prompted the
definition of several equational logics that we introduced in
[
5
]
as two-valued

short-circuit logics
(SCLs). For example,
x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x=x
is refuted in
Free SCL (
FSCL
), while it holds in Memorising SCL (
MSCL
).
Next, Static SCL (
SSCL
) is the short-circuit logic that represents propositional logic
with connectives
{~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}}
and
{~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}}
instead of
\wedge
and
\vee
, and thus with short-circuit
evaluation as the prescribed evaluation strategy.
For example,
x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}y=y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x
is refuted in

MSCL
, while it holds in
in
SSCL
.
1
1

1

In
[
5
]
, five different, two-valued SCLs are defined, of which
FSCL
is the weakest and

SSCL
, a sequential version of propositional logic, is the strongest.

These SCLs can be ordered by a chain
\textup{$\textrm{FSCL}$}\prec\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}\prec\textup{$\textrm{SSCL}$}
:

\displaystyle\textup{$\textrm{FSCL}$}\vdash s=t

\displaystyle~\Longrightarrow~\mathcal{L}\vdash s=t\quad\text{with $\mathcal{L}$ any SCL},

\displaystyle\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}\vdash s=t

\displaystyle~\Longrightarrow~\textup{$\textrm{SSCL}$}\vdash s=t.

The logic
FSCL
is immune to side effects: in FSCL,
x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x=x

does not hold because a side effect occurring at the first evaluation of an atom
(propositional variable)
may alter its second
evaluation result, while
MSCL
represents the setting in which
the evaluation of each atom in an expression is memorised, so that
(atomic) side effects cannot occur.
In
MSCL
, the entire process of evaluation is distinctive and therefore
{~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}}
is not commutative,
e.g.,
x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F}
requires evaluation of
x
, while
{\sf F}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x
does not.
Examples of laws of
FSCL
(and thus of
MSCL
) are

\neg\neg x=x,~~{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x=x,~~{\sf F}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x={\sf F}

and

(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}y)~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}z=x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}z)
.

In
MSCL
, the connective
{~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}}
does not depend on the presence of the constant

{\sf F}
because

\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}\vdash x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y=(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}y)~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x).

We observe that
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}
can be seen as a two-valued variant of
CL
that axiomatises
a short-circuit logic that resides in between
MSCL
and
SSCL
, which we will define as

C
\ell
SCL
2
(two-valued Conditional SCL).
A simple and interesting example that distinguishes
MSCL
and
C
\ell
SCL
2
is the equation

x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y=y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}x,

which holds in the latter and is refuted in
MSCL
.
Because
C
\ell
SCL
2
refutes
x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}y=y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x
, it follows that

\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}\prec\textup{$\textrm{C$\ell$SCL${}_{2}$}$}\prec\textup{$\textrm{SSCL}$}
.

In
[
6
]
we defined three-valued versions of
FSCL
and
MSCL
, denoted
\textup{$\textrm{FSCL}$}^{\sf U}
and
\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}^{\sf U}
, respectively.

\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{3}
axiomatises
C
\ell
SCL
, the three-valued version of
C
\ell
SCL
2
,
and
\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}^{\sf U}\prec\textup{$\textrm{C$\ell$SCL}$}
follows from the same example.
(Of course,
SSCL
has no extension with
{\sf U}
because commutativity of
{~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}}
implies

{\sf U}={\sf U}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F}={\sf F}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf U}={\sf F}
.)

We continue with a brief introduction to the building blocks for our generic definition
of short-circuit logics.
In 1985, Hoare introduced in
[
13
]
the
conditional
, a ternary connective with
notation
x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}z
that models
if
y
then
x
else
z
, and
provided eleven equational axioms to show that the conditional and two constants for
truth and falsehood characterise the propositional calculus.
With the conditional, algebraic properties
can be elegantly expressed, e.g.,
{\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F}=x

and
x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}(y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}z\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}u)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}v=(x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}v)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}z\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}(x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}u\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}v)
.
However, the same result was proved in 1948 by Church
in
[
8
]
, where he introduced
conditioned disjunction
,
2
2

2

For the conditioned disjunction, reference
[
9
]
(1956)
is often used, and also the name
conditional disjunction
.

notation
[x,y,z]
, that also models
if
y
then
x
else
z
.
Moreover, in
[
8
]
, the duality property of
[x,y,z]

is highlighted and even older related work is mentioned, a quote:

“
Conditioned disjunction,
t
, and
f
, are
a complete set of independent primitive connectives for the propositional calculus.
This has
been proved by E. L. Post as a part of his comprehensive study of primitive connectives for the
propositional calculus. [footnote: E. L. Post.

The Two-Valued Iterative Systems of Mathematical Logic
(Annals
of Mathematics Studies, no. 5), Princeton, N. J., 1941.] But it seems worth while to give a separate
proof here. [footnote: The method of the proof of completeness is that used by E. L. Post in 1921 in connection
with a different set of primitive connectives.
See the
American Journal of Mathematics
, vol. 43, pp. 167-168.]”

[Our comment:
the mentioned “method of the proof” is a simple induction with respect to the number of variables
of a possible connective.]

Both
[
13
]
and
[
8
]
do not address the fact that the ternary connective defined therein
in fact prescribes short-circuit evaluation.

In
[
3
]
, we introduced
proposition algebra
, motivated
by the observation that Hoare’s conditional both naturally prescribes short-circuit evaluation
and admits several semantics (valuation congruences), the least distinguishing of which is that
of sequential propositional logic.
Furthermore,

x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}y=y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F}

can be seen as a formal definition of what is usually described as
the short-circuit evaluation of conjunction.
In
[
5
]
, we used proposition algebra as an algebraic framework to define several short-circuit logics,
including
FSCL
,
MSCL
and
SSCL
, and in
[
6
]

to define
\textup{$\textrm{FSCL}$}^{\sf U}
and
\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}^{\sf U}
, i.e. the three-valued variants of
FSCL
and
MSCL
.

Contents of the paper.

-
In Section
2
, we recall the definitions of
the proposition algebras
CP
and
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{mem}}
that underlie the definitions of
FSCL
and
MSCL
, respectively.
We also recall the three-valued variants.

-
In Section
3
, we define the two-valued proposition algebra
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}
which is related to
conditional logic.
We define and explain in detail so-called ‘CL-basic forms’, based on the mem-basic forms for
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{mem}}
, which
resemble the “reduced trees” defined in
[
11
]
that provide a
“standard model” for free
C
-algebras.
We prove that
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}
completely axiomatises
C
\ell
SCL
2
, the SCL defined by
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}
and the short-circuit connectives.

-
In Section
4
, we define the three-valued proposition algebra
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}^{\sf U}
and do the same as in
Section
3
for the three-valued variants.
It follows that
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{3}
is a complete axiomatisation of
C
\ell
SCL
.

-
In Section
5
, we show that the axiomatisation of CL in Table
1
is not
independent and provide several axiomatisations of
CL
,
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}
,
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}^{\sf U}
(i.e.
CL
with only
{\sf U}
),
and
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{3}
that are independent.

-
In Section
6
,
we consider the distinguishing example

x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y=y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}x
that proves
\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}^{\sf U}\prec\textup{$\textrm{C$\ell$SCL}$}

and show that in
C
\ell
SCL
, and thus in
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{3}
,
the (definable) full left-sequential connectives and negation
define Bochvar’s three-valued strict logic.
We conclude with a comment on the role of the constants
{\sf T}
and
{\sf F}
in SCLs and some remarks on
related and future work.

2
Proposition algebra and short-circuit logics

In this section we recall the definitions of two-valued and three-valued proposition algebra, and
of the short-circuit logics mentioned in the previous section.

\displaystyle\textup{$\textrm{CP}$}:

\displaystyle x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}{\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}y

\displaystyle=x

(CP1)

\displaystyle x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}{\sf F}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}y

\displaystyle=y

(CP2)

\displaystyle{\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F}

\displaystyle=x

(CP3)

\displaystyle x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}(y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}z\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}u)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}v

\displaystyle=(x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}v)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}z\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}(x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}u\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}v)

(CP4)

\displaystyle\textup{$\textrm{CP}$}^{\sf U}:

\displaystyle x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}{\sf U}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}y

\displaystyle={\sf U}

(CP-U)

Table 2:
CP
, a set of basic axioms of proposition algebra,
and
\textup{$\textrm{CP}$}^{\sf U}=\textup{$\textrm{CP}$}\cup\eqref{cpu}

Definition 2.1
.

For
A
a set of atoms (propositional variables), define the signatures

\Sigma_{\textup{$\textrm{CP}$}}(A)=\{\_\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}\_\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}\_,{\sf T},{\sf F},a\mid a\in A\}\quad\text{and}\quad\Sigma_{\textup{$\textrm{CP}$}}^{\sf U}(A)=\Sigma_{\textup{$\textrm{CP}$}}(A)\cup\{{\sf U}\}

and the sets
{\mathcal{C}_{A}}
and
{\mathcal{C}_{A}^{\sf U}}
of
conditional expressions

as the set of closed terms over
\Sigma_{\textup{$\textrm{CP}$}}(A)
and
\Sigma_{\textup{$\textrm{CP}$}}^{\sf U}(A)
, respectively.

In Table
2
we display
CP
, a set of basic axioms for the conditional
defined in
[
3
]
, and
\textup{$\textrm{CP}$}^{\sf U}
, which was defined in
[
6
]
.
The dual of a conditional expression
P
, notation
P^{d}
, is defined by
{\sf T}^{d}={\sf F}
,
{\sf F}^{d}={\sf T}
,

{\sf U}^{d}={\sf U}
,

a^{d}=a
for
A\in A
,
and
(P\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}Q\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}R)^{d}=R^{d}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}Q^{d}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}P^{d}
. Taking the dual
of a variable
x
as itself, it easily follows that
CP
and
\textup{$\textrm{CP}$}^{\sf U}
are self-dual axiomatisations.

The axioms of
\textup{$\textrm{CP}$}^{\sf U}
define a congruence over
{\mathcal{C}_{A}^{\sf U}}
:

free valuation congruence
, which can be characterised
with help of basic forms.
3
3

3

About terminology: 1) Two-valued free valuation congruence (FVC) was defined in
[
3
]

in terms of “valuation algebras”, hence the name.
2)
We use “basic form” rather than “normal form” because
the “natural” normal form of
{\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F}
would be
a
, while the basic form
of atom
a
is
{\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F}
.

Definition 2.2
.

Basic forms

are defined by the following grammar

t::={\sf T}\mid{\sf F}\mid{\sf U}\mid t\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}t\quad\text{for $a\in A$.}

We write
\textit{BF}_{A}^{\>{\sf U}}
for the set of basic forms over
A
, and
\textit{BF}_{A}
for the subset of

\textit{BF}_{A}^{\>{\sf U}}
in which
{\sf U}
does not occur.
The constans in a basic form
P\in\textit{BF}_{A}^{\>{\sf U}}
are called
leaves
.
The
depth

d(P)
of
P\in\textit{BF}_{A}^{\>{\sf U}}
is defined by

d({\sf T})=d({\sf F})=d({\sf U})=0
and

d(Q\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}R)=1+\max\{d(Q),d(R)\}
.

In
[
6
, Appendix A8]
we prove that for each
P\in{\mathcal{C}_{A}^{\sf U}}
there is a unique
Q\in\textit{BF}_{A}^{\>{\sf U}}
such that

\textup{$\textrm{CP}$}^{\sf U}\vdash P=Q
.
This implies that for all
P,Q\in{\mathcal{C}_{A}^{\sf U}},~\textup{$\textrm{CP}$}^{\sf U}\vdash P=Q
if, and only if,
P
and
Q
have the same (unique)
basic form, that is, if
P
and
Q
are free valuation congruent.

Memorising valuation congruence
is
defined by
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{mem}}^{\sf U}
, i.e.,
\textup{$\textrm{CP}$}^{\sf U}
extended with the axiom

\displaystyle x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}(z\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}u\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}(v\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}w))=x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}(z\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}u\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}w),

(CPmem)

which expresses that the evaluation of
y
is memorised. Furthermore, in
[
5
]
,

\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{mem}}
is defined as the extension of
CP
with (
CPmem
).

By replacing
y

by
{\sf F}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf T}
and
u
by
{\sf F}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}u\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf T}
,
it follows that

\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{mem}}^{\sf U}\vdash((w\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}v)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}u\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}z)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}z=(w\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}u\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}z)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}x,

which is the dual of the above axiom, so the duality principle holds in
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{mem}}^{\sf U}
.

Definition 2.3
.

The constants
{\sf T}
,
{\sf F}
and
{\sf U}
are
mem-basic forms
, and
a basic form
P\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}Q
is a

mem-basic form
if
a
does not occur in
P
and
Q
.

We write
\textit{MBF}_{A}^{\,{\sf U}}
for the set of basic forms over
A
, and
\textit{MBF}_{A}
for the subset of

\textit{MBF}_{A}^{\,{\sf U}}
in which
{\sf U}
does not occur.

Definition 2.4
.

For
a\in A
, the auxiliary functions
\ell_{a},r_{a}:\textit{BF}_{A}^{\>{\sf U}}\to\textit{BF}_{A}^{\>{\sf U}}
, “left
a
-reduction” and “right
a
-reduction”,
are defined by

\ell_{a}({\sf T})={\sf T},~~\ell_{a}({\sf F})={\sf F},~~\ell_{a}({\sf U})={\sf U}
,

r_{a}({\sf T})={\sf T},~~r_{a}({\sf F})={\sf F},~~r_{a}({\sf U})={\sf U}
,

\displaystyle\ell_{a}(P\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}b\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}Q)=\begin{cases}P&\text{if $b=a$},\\
\ell_{a}(P)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}b\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}\ell_{a}(Q)&\text{if $b\neq a$},\end{cases}\quad

\displaystyle r_{a}(P\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}b\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}Q)=\begin{cases}Q&\text{if $b=a$},\\
r_{a}(P)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}b\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}r_{a}(Q)&\text{if $b\neq a$}.\end{cases}

So, for each
P\in\textit{MBF}_{A}^{\,{\sf U}}
, both
\ell_{a}(P)
and
r_{a}(P)
are mem-basic forms that do not contain
a
.
Mem-basic forms and the functions
\ell_{a}()
and
r_{a}()
are a point of departure in the next section.

In
[
4
, Thm.5.7]
we proved a result that implies that for each
P\in{\mathcal{C}_{A}}
,
there is a unique
Q\in\textit{MBF}_{A}
such that
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{mem}}\vdash P=Q
,
and for all
P,Q\in{\mathcal{C}_{A}},~\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{mem}}\vdash P=Q
if, and only if,
P
and
Q
have the same (unique)
mem-basic form, that is, if
P
and
Q
are memorising valuation congruent.
Given the approach in
[
6
]
,
the same results follow for the three-valued setting.

Definition 2.5
.

For
A
a set of atoms (propositional variables), define the signatures

\Sigma_{\textup{$\textrm{SCL}$}}(A)=\{~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}},~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}},\neg,{\sf T},{\sf F},a\mid a\in A\}\quad\text{and}\quad\Sigma_{\textup{$\textrm{SCL}$}}^{\sf U}(A)=\Sigma_{\textup{$\textrm{SCL}$}}(A)\cup\{{\sf U}\}

and the sets
{\mathcal{S}_{A}}
and
{\mathcal{S}_{A}^{\sf U}}
of
sequential expressions

as the set of closed terms over
\Sigma_{\textup{$\textrm{SCL}$}}(A)
and
\Sigma_{\textup{$\textrm{SCL}$}}^{\sf U}(A)
, respectively.

The short-circuit connectives are defined in
\textup{$\textrm{CP}$}^{\sf U}
(and in
CP
) by the following axioms:

\displaystyle\neg x

\displaystyle={\sf F}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf T},

(defNeg)

\displaystyle x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}y

\displaystyle=y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F},

(defAnd)

and, by duality, short-circuit disjunction should satisfy in
\textup{$\textrm{CP}$}^{\sf U}
(and in
CP
)

x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}y={\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}y,

(defOr)

which we show below.
In a similar way it can be shown that full left-sequential conjunction, which we already defined by

x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y=(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F}))~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}y
,
satisfies

x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y=y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}({\sf F}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F}).

In
[
5
]
we defined (two-valued) short-circuit logics in a generic way with help of the
conditional by restricting the consequences of a particular
CP
-axiomatisation extended with
equations (
defNeg
) and (
defAnd
) to the signature
\Sigma_{\textup{$\textrm{SCL}$}}(A)
,
and we repeat here some of these definitions.
So, the conditional connective is considered a hidden operator.
In these definitions, the export operator
\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.8,0.0)\put(-0.5,1.6){\line{1}{0}{1.4}}\put(-0.5,-0.2){\line{1}{0}{1.4}}\put(-0.44,-0.2){\line{0}{1}{1.8}}\put(0.84,-0.2){\line{0}{1}{1.8}}\end{picture}}
of Module algebra
[
2
]

is used to express hiding in a concise way:
in module algebra,
S\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.8,0.0)\put(-0.5,1.6){\line{1}{0}{1.4}}\put(-0.5,-0.2){\line{1}{0}{1.4}}\put(-0.44,-0.2){\line{0}{1}{1.8}}\put(0.84,-0.2){\line{0}{1}{1.8}}\end{picture}}X
is the operation that
exports the signature
S
from module
X
while declaring
other signature elements hidden.

Definition 2.6
.

A
short-circuit logic

is a logic that implies the consequences
of the module expression

\displaystyle\textup{$\textrm{SCL}$}=\{{\sf T},\neg,~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\}\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.8,0.0)\put(-0.5,1.6){\line{1}{0}{1.4}}\put(-0.5,-0.2){\line{1}{0}{1.4}}\put(-0.44,-0.2){\line{0}{1}{1.8}}\put(0.84,-0.2){\line{0}{1}{1.8}}\end{picture}}(

\displaystyle\textup{$\textrm{CP}$}\cup\{\eqref{defneg},\eqref{defand}\}).

Definition 2.7
.

Free short-circuit logic
(\textup{$\textrm{FSCL}$})

is the short-circuit logic that implies no other
consequences than those of the module expression
SCL
.

Memorising short-circuit logic
(\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$})

is the short-circuit logic that implies no other
consequences than those of the module expression

\{{\sf T},\neg,~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\}\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.8,0.0)\put(-0.5,1.6){\line{1}{0}{1.4}}\put(-0.5,-0.2){\line{1}{0}{1.4}}\put(-0.44,-0.2){\line{0}{1}{1.8}}\put(0.84,-0.2){\line{0}{1}{1.8}}\end{picture}}(\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{mem}}\cup\{\eqref{defneg},\eqref{defand}\}).

Static short-circuit logic
(\textup{$\textrm{SSCL}$})

is the short-circuit logic that implies no other
consequences than those of the module expression

\{{\sf T},\neg,~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\}\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.8,0.0)\put(-0.5,1.6){\line{1}{0}{1.4}}\put(-0.5,-0.2){\line{1}{0}{1.4}}\put(-0.44,-0.2){\line{0}{1}{1.8}}\put(0.84,-0.2){\line{0}{1}{1.8}}\end{picture}}(\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{mem}}\cup\{{\sf F}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F}={\sf F},\eqref{defneg},\eqref{defand}\}).

To enhance readability, we extend these short-circuit logics with the constant
{\sf F}
and its
defining axiom
{\sf F}=\neg{\sf T}
,
which is justified by the
SCL
-derivation

\displaystyle{\sf F}

\displaystyle={\sf F}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}{\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf T}

by (
CP1
)

\displaystyle=\neg{\sf T},

by (
defNeg
)

and with the connective
~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}
and its defining
axiom
x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}y=\neg(\neg x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg y)
, which is justified by

\displaystyle\neg(\neg x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg y)

Table 3:
The set
EqMSCL
of axioms for
MSCL

The equational logic
EqMSCL
is defined by the five axioms in Table
3
.
In
[
6
]
it is proved that

axiomatises

.

Some consequences of
EqMSCL
are these (see
[
6
]
):

(i)

the connective
~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}
is associative, i.e.
(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}y)~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}z=x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}z)
,

(ii)

the connective
~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}
is idempotent, i.e.
x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x=x
,

(iii)

the connective
~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}
is left-distributive, i.e.

x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}z)=(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}y)~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}z)
, and

(iv)

\neg\neg x=x
,
x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg x=\neg x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x
, and

.

Finally, we defined in
[
6
]
the short-circuit logics
\textup{$\textrm{FSCL}$}^{\sf U}
and
\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}^{\sf U}
by using
\textup{$\textrm{CP}$}^{\sf U}
and
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{mem}}^{\sf U}
, respectively, instead of their two-valued variants, and exporting
{\sf U}
.
We proved that
MSCL
and
\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}^{\sf U}
are axiomatised by the equational logics
EqMSCL
and
\textup{$\textrm{EqMSCL}$}^{\sf U}=\textup{$\textrm{EqMSCL}$}\cup\{\neg{\sf U}={\sf U}\}
, respectively.

3
Conditional short-circuit logic, the two-valued case

In this section we restrict to the two-valued setting. In particular, where we write about
mem-basic forms, we refer to those in
\textit{MBF}_{A}
(see Definition
2.3
).
We define ‘CL-basic forms’ based on the mem-basic forms for
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{mem}}
and the short-circuit logic
C
\ell
SCL
2

and prove that
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}
completely axiomatises
C
\ell
SCL
2
, the two-valued SCL defined by
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}
and the short-circuit connectives.

Consider the axiom

\displaystyle{\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}(y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}(x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F})={\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}(x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}(y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F}),

(CL1)

i.e. the translation of the

(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}y)~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x)=(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x)~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}y)

Lemma 3.1

(1)

\displaystyle=x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}(y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}u\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}(x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}(u\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}v)

\displaystyle=(x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}(x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}(u\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}v))\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}u\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}(x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}(u\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}v)

\displaystyle=(x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}((x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}u\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}v)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}v))\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}u\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}((x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}u\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}v)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}v)

\displaystyle=(x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}v)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}u\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}(v\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}v),

(2)

and apply axiom (
CL1
) in (
1
), so exchange
u
and
y
. By (
2
),

(x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}v)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}u\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}(v\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}v)=(x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}u\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}v)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}(v\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}u\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}v).

Now replace
v
by
v\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}(z\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}u\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}w)
and apply memorisation:

(x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}z)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}u\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}(v\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}w)=(x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}u\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}v)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}(z\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}u\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}w).

(ii)
Derive
{\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}(y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}(x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F})=({\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}({\sf F}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F})

and apply (
CL2
).
∎

Definition 3.2
.

Let
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}
be the extension of
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{mem}}
with axiom (
CL2
).

Conditional short-circuit logic
(\textup{$\textrm{C$\ell$SCL${}_{2}$}$})

is the short-circuit logic that implies no other
consequences than those of the module expression

\{{\sf T},\neg,~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\}\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.8,0.0)\put(-0.5,1.6){\line{1}{0}{1.4}}\put(-0.5,-0.2){\line{1}{0}{1.4}}\put(-0.44,-0.2){\line{0}{1}{1.8}}\put(0.84,-0.2){\line{0}{1}{1.8}}\end{picture}}(\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}\cup\{\eqref{defneg},\eqref{defand}\}).

Observe that the axiom (
CL2
) is self-dual, so the duality principle holds in
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}
.
In the setting of CL, we shall adopt a strict total ordering
<
of
A
, in order to
deal with the axiom (
CL2
).
Whenever we want to be explicit about this ordering, we write

(A,<).

Below we define CL-basic forms and prove that
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}\vdash P=Q
if, and only if,
P
and
Q

are
conditional valuation congruent
, that is, if
P
and
Q

have the same unique CL-basic form (relative to a strict total ordering of
A
).

Example 3.3
.

Consider the mem-basic form
\textit{Input}=P\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}Q
with

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle=(({\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}d\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}c\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}({\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}d\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F}))\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}b\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}(({\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}c\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}d\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf T}),

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=({\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}d\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}c\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}({\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}d\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F}).

This example is a first step to our definition of so-called
CL-basic forms
,
a proper subset of the mem-basic forms.
End example.

We discuss the question of how mem-basic forms can be assigned a
unique representation modulo conditional valuation congruence, where this representation itself must of
course be a mem-basic form.
In the following recursive definition, the “shared alphabet” of a mem-basic form
is the set of atoms that occur in
each
evaluation path
(whereas the “alphabet”
\alpha(P)
of a mem-basic form
P
is the set of all its atoms, i.e.
those that occur in at least one evaluation path).

Definition 3.4
.

For a mem-basic form
P
, its

shared alphabet

\widetilde{\alpha}(P)

is the set defined by

\widetilde{\alpha}({\sf T})=\widetilde{\alpha}({\sf F})=\emptyset,\quad\widetilde{\alpha}(P_{1}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}P_{2})=\{a\}\cup(\widetilde{\alpha}(P_{1}))\cap\widetilde{\alpha}(P_{2})).

Hence,
\widetilde{\alpha}(P)=\emptyset
iff
P\in\{{\sf T},{\sf F}\}
, and if
P=P_{1}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}P_{2}
, then
a\in\widetilde{\alpha}(P)
.
In Example
3.3
,
\widetilde{\alpha}(\textit{Input})=\{a,d\}
,
and this is also the shared alphabet of the other mem-basic forms.

Lemma 3.5
.

If
P
is a mem-basic form and
a\in\widetilde{\alpha}(P)
, then
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}\vdash P=\ell_{a}(P)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}r_{a}(P)
.

Proof.

By structural induction on
P
.
If
P=P_{1}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}P_{2}
, then
\ell_{a}(P)=P_{1}
and
r_{a}(P)=P_{2}
, so we are done, and
if
P=P_{1}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}b\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}P_{2}
with
b\neq a
, then
a\in(\widetilde{\alpha}(P_{1})\cap\widetilde{\alpha}(P_{2}))
, hence

\displaystyle\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}\vdash

\displaystyle=P_{1}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}b\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}P_{2}

\displaystyle=(\ell_{a}(P_{1})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}r_{a}(P_{1}))\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}b\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}(\ell_{a}(P_{2})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}r_{a}(P_{2}))

(by induction)

\displaystyle=(\ell_{a}(P_{1})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}b\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}\ell_{a}(P_{2}))\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}(r_{a}(P_{1})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}b\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}r_{a}(P_{2}))

(by axiom (
CL2
))

\displaystyle=\ell_{a}(P)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}r_{a}(P).

∎

Lemma
3.5
implies the following consequence, which shows that
\widetilde{\alpha}()
is preserved under
provable equality:

Lemma 3.6
.

If
P,Q
are mem-basic forms and
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}\vdash P=Q
, then
\widetilde{\alpha}(P)=\widetilde{\alpha}(Q)
.

Proof.

If
a\in\widetilde{\alpha}(P)
, then
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}\vdash P=\ell_{a}(P)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}r_{a}(P)
, so

\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}\vdash Q=\ell_{a}(P)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}r_{a}(P)
and thus
a\in\widetilde{\alpha}(Q)
. By symmetry,
\widetilde{\alpha}(P)=\widetilde{\alpha}(Q)
.
∎

To decide whether different mem-basic forms are provably equal in
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}
, we define so-called
CL-basic forms in a recursive way.
We first define some technical notions that are used in these basic forms.

Definition 3.7
.

For
A
a nonempty set of atoms,
A^{s}
is the set of words over
A
with the property that each

\sigma\in A^{s}
contains no multiple occurrences of the same atom. We write
\epsilon

for the empty string, thus
\epsilon\in A^{s}
. Moreover, we define
\emptyset^{s}=\{\epsilon\}
.

For
\sigma\in A^{s}
with length
|\sigma|=n
and
P_{0},...,P_{2^{n}-1}
mem-basic forms, define
F_{\sigma}(P_{0},...,P_{2^{n}-1})
by

\displaystyle\sigma=\epsilon:

\displaystyle F_{\sigma}(P_{0})=P_{0},

\displaystyle\sigma=a\rho,~a\in A:

\displaystyle F_{\sigma}(P_{0},...,P_{2^{n}-1})=F_{\rho}(P_{0},...,P_{2^{|\rho|}-1})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}F_{\rho}(P_{2^{|\rho|}},...,P_{2^{n}-1}).

We say that

\sigma\in A^{s}

agrees with

(A,<)
if
\sigma
’s atoms occur in increasing order.

Definition 3.8
.

A mem-basic form
P\in\textit{MBF}_{A}
is a
CL-basic form
if
for the unique
\sigma\in A^{s}
of length
|\widetilde{\alpha}(P)|

that agrees with
(A,<)
,

P=F_{\sigma}(P_{0},...,P_{2^{|\sigma|}-1})
and each

P_{i}
is a CL-basic form.

Observe that a mem-basic form
P
is a CL-basic form if axiom (
CL2
) cannot be applied to
P

(i.e. if
|\widetilde{\alpha}(Q)|\leq 1
for all subterms
Q
of
P
), e.g. as in

({\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}b\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}({\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}c\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F}).

Notation and convention.

For
A^{\prime}
a finite set of atoms
we can
represent
(A^{\prime},<)
by the string
\rho\in(A^{\prime})^{s}
that contains all atoms of
A^{\prime}

in increasing order (thus
|\rho|=|A^{\prime}|
).
We will often just write
Cbf
instead of “CL-basic form”.

In Example
3.3
, the terms displayed in the right column are Cbfs if

adbc
agrees with
(A,<)
or if
dacb
does, respectively,
and those in the left column are not
because they do not satisfy the condition
P=F_{ad}(...)
or
P=F_{da}(...)
, respectively.
We give another example that further illustrates the role of
(A,<)
and provides more intuition
about the existence of unique Cbfs.

Example 3.9
.

Consider the following mem-basic form
P
, which we display graphically:

a
b
c
e
d
{\sf T}
{\sf F}
d
{\sf T}
{\sf F}
{\sf F}
c
{\sf T}
{\sf F}
d
e
{\sf T}
{\sf F}
e
{\sf F}
{\sf T}

Clearly,
P
is a mem-basic form and
\widetilde{\alpha}(P)=\{a\}
.
Observe that some subterms have overlap and can be rearranged according to axiom (
CL2
).
To obtain a unique CL-basic form, the two outer subterms
({\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}d\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}e\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}({\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}d\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F})

and
({\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}e\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}d\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}({\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}e\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F})

should be made uniform and it must be decided whether
b
and the two adjacent
c
’s
should be interchanged (with rearranged subterms) or not.

If
abcde
agrees with
(A,<)
, the left subterm
({\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}d\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}e\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}({\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}d\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F})
should be replaced by

({\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}e\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf T})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}d\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}({\sf F}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}e\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F}),

and if
acbde
agrees with
(A,<)
, the left subterm
Q\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}b\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}R
should be replaced by one with root
c

that we display graphically:

c
b
d
e
{\sf T}
{\sf T}
e
{\sf F}
{\sf F}
{\sf T}
b
{\sf F}
{\sf F}

Note that each alternative ordering
(A,<^{\prime})
determines how
P
should be transformed into a provably
equal Cbf because it determines whether
b<^{\prime}c
or
c<^{\prime}b
and
d<^{\prime}e
or
e<^{\prime}d
, and
that
a
is the only possible root atom of such a transformation.
End example.

For
P
a mem-basic form different from
{\sf T}
or
{\sf F}
,
a consequence of axiom (
CL2
) concerns a provably equal representation that is ordered
using
\widetilde{\alpha}(P)
.

Lemma 3.10
.

For each mem-basic form
P\in\textit{MBF}_{A}

and
\sigma\in(\widetilde{\alpha}(P))^{s}
that has maximal length and agrees with
(A,<)
,
there exist mem-basic forms
P_{0},...,P_{2^{|\sigma|}-1}
such that

\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}\vdash P=F_{\sigma}(P_{0},...,P_{2^{|\sigma|}-1}).

Proof.

By induction on the depth of
P
. The base case
d(P)=0
is trivial.
If
d(P)\geq 1
and

\sigma=a\rho
, then
\rho
represents the ordering of the shared alphabets of
the mem-basic forms
\ell_{a}(P)
and
r_{a}(P)
that
are both of lesser depth, hence

\displaystyle\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}\vdash P

\displaystyle=\ell_{a}(P)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}r_{a}(P)

(by Lemma
3.5
)

\displaystyle=F_{\rho}(P_{0},...,P_{2^{|\rho|}-1})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}F_{\rho}(P_{0}^{\prime},...,P_{2^{|\rho|}-1}^{\prime})

(IH)

\displaystyle=F_{\sigma}(P_{0},...,P_{2^{|\sigma|}-1})

if
P_{2^{|\rho|}+i}=P_{i}^{\prime}
for
i=0,...,2^{|\rho|}-1
.
∎

Theorem 3.11
.

For each mem-basic form
P\in\textit{MBF}_{A}
there is a unique
CL-basic form
Q
such that
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}\vdash P=Q
.

Proof.

By induction on the depth of
P
.
If
d(P)=0
then
P\in\{{\sf T},{\sf F}\}
and we are done.

If
d(P)>0
then
|\widetilde{\alpha}(P)|=n
for some
n\geq 1
.
For the unique
\sigma\in(\widetilde{\alpha}(P))^{s}
of length
n
that agrees with
(A,<)

and Lemma
3.10
,

\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}\vdash P=F_{\sigma}(P_{0},...,P_{2^{n}-1})
.
By induction, there are unique Cbfs
Q_{i}
such that
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}\vdash P_{i}=Q_{i}
.
Hence,
Q=F_{\sigma}(Q_{0},...,Q_{2^{n}-1})
is the unique
Cbf that satisfies
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}\vdash P=Q
.
∎

In
[
4
, Thm.5.7]
we prove that for each
P\in{\mathcal{C}_{A}}
, there is a unique mem-basic form obtained
by a normalisation function
\mathit{mbf}()
such that

\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{mem}}\vdash P=\mathit{mbf}(P)
. Hence, we find the following result:

Corollary 3.12
.

For all
P,Q\in{\mathcal{C}_{A}}
,

\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}\vdash P=Q

if, and only if,

\llbracket P\rrbracket_{\textit{cl}}=\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\textit{cl}},\text{where $\llbracket P\rrbracket_{\textit{cl}}$ is $P$'s unique CL-basic form}.

Corollary
3.12
and the above theorem imply that

\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}
axiomatises two-valued
conditional valuation congruence, that is, the congruence defined by having equal CL-basic forms.

It remains to be shown that
C
\ell
SCL
2
is completely axiomatised by the equational logic
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}
.
Given a signature
\Sigma
, we write

\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma,\cal X}

for the set of open terms over

\Sigma
with variables in
\cal X
.
As in
[
6
]
, we define the following translation functions:

f:\mathbb{T}_{{\Sigma_{\textup{$\textrm{SCL}$}}(A)},\cal X}\to\mathbb{T}_{{\Sigma_{\textup{$\textrm{CP}$}}(A)},\cal X}

by

\displaystyle f(B)

\displaystyle=bl~\text{ for }B\in\{{\sf T},{\sf F}\},

\displaystyle f(\neg t)

\displaystyle={\sf F}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}f(t)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf T},

\displaystyle f(a)

\displaystyle=a~\text{ for }a\in A,

\displaystyle f(t_{1}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}t_{2})

\displaystyle=f(t_{2})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}f(t_{1})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F},

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=x~\text{ for }x\in\cal X,

\displaystyle f(t_{1}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}t_{2})

\displaystyle={\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}f(t_{1})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}f(t_{2}).\hskip 91.04881pt

g:\mathbb{T}_{{\Sigma_{\textup{$\textrm{CP}$}}(A)},\cal X}\to\mathbb{T}_{{\Sigma_{\textup{$\textrm{SCL}$}}(A)},\cal X}

by

\displaystyle g(B)

\displaystyle=B~\text{ for }B\in\{{\sf T},{\sf F}\},

\displaystyle g(x)

\displaystyle=x~\text{ for }x\in\cal X,

\displaystyle g(a)

\displaystyle=a~\text{ for }a\in A,

\displaystyle g(t_{1}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}t_{2}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}t_{3})

\displaystyle=(g(t_{2})~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}g(t_{1}))~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}(\neg g(t_{2})~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}g(t_{3})).

Theorem 3.13
.

For all terms
s,t
over
\Sigma_{\textup{$\textrm{SCL}$}}(A)
,
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}\vdash s=t~\iff~\textup{$\textrm{C$\ell$SCL${}_{2}$}$}\vdash s=t
.

Proof.

In
[
6
, Thm.6.5]
it is proved that for all terms
s,t
over
\Sigma_{\textup{$\textrm{SCL}$}}(A)
,

\textup{$\textrm{EqMSCL}$}\vdash s=t~\iff~\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}\vdash s=t

and the two crucial intermediate results in this proof, when lifted to the present case, are

1) for all
s,t\in\mathbb{T}_{{\Sigma_{\textup{$\textrm{CP}$}}(A)},\cal X}
,
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}\vdash s=t~\Rightarrow~\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}\vdash g(s)=g(t)
, and

2) for all
t\in\mathbb{T}_{{\Sigma_{\textup{$\textrm{SCL}$}}(A)},\cal X}
,
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}\vdash g(f(t))=t
.

For result 1 it must be shown that the axioms of
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}
are derivable from
C
\ell
SCL
2
.
By Theorem
5.2
,
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}\vdash\textup{$\textrm{EqMSCL}$}
, so it remains to be shown
that axiom (
CL2
) is derivable from
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}
, and this follows from
Lemma
3.1
because axiom (
CL1
) is the

g()
-translation of axiom (
1.1g
).
Result 2 holds for
EqMSCL
, and hence for
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}
.
∎

Finally, we can use CL-basic forms to define “normal forms” in the equational theory of
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}
:
4
4

4

The term “normal form” is not used in
[
11
]
, but Guzmán’s follow-up
paper
[
12
]
states that the completeness of a Gentzen system for
conditional logic
“relies heavily on the axiomatization
of the equational
theory of
C
, and on the normal form in this equational theory; see
[
11
]
.
The author believes that an understanding of this normal form, as presented in section 3 of
[
11
]
,
is essential in order to see through the formalisms in the proofs of (2.3), (2.7) and (2.10).”

for
P\in{\mathcal{S}_{A}}
we find
f(P)\in{\mathcal{C}_{A}}
,
and thus, given an ordering
(A,<)
, a unique CL-basic form
\mathit{mbf}(f(P))
such that

\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}\vdash f(P)=\mathit{mbf}(f(P))
and thus

\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}\vdash P=g(f(P))=g(\mathit{mbf}(f(P))),

so
g(\mathit{mbf}(f(P)))
can be defined as the normal form of
P
.

4
Conditional short-circuit logic, the three-valued case

In this section we consider the three-valued setting and start from mem-basic forms that may contain
{\sf U}
,
thus the set
\textit{MBF}_{A}^{\,{\sf U}}
of mem-basic forms.
We proceed as in Section
3
and it will appear that the CL-basic forms with
{\sf U}
are a bit more complex.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}^{\sf U}
be the extension of
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}
with axiom (
CP-U
), i.e.
x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}{\sf U}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}y={\sf U}
.

Conditional short-circuit logic with undefinedness
(\textup{$\textrm{C$\ell$SCL}$})

is the short-circuit logic that implies no other
consequences than those of the module expression

\{{\sf T},{\sf U},\neg,~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\}\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.8,0.0)\put(-0.5,1.6){\line{1}{0}{1.4}}\put(-0.5,-0.2){\line{1}{0}{1.4}}\put(-0.44,-0.2){\line{0}{1}{1.8}}\put(0.84,-0.2){\line{0}{1}{1.8}}\end{picture}}(\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}^{\sf U}\cup\{\eqref{defneg},\eqref{defand}\}).

A typical derivation and a typical example in
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}^{\sf U}
:

\displaystyle\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}^{\sf U}\vdash{\sf U}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf U}

\displaystyle=(y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}{\sf U}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}z)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}(v\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}{\sf U}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}w)

\displaystyle=(y\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}v)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}{\sf U}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}(z\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}x\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}w)

\displaystyle={\sf U},

\displaystyle\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}^{\sf U}\vdash({\sf F}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf U})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}b\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}({\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf U})

\displaystyle=({\sf F}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}b\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf T})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}({\sf U}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}b\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf U})

\displaystyle=({\sf F}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}b\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf T})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf U}.

This example motivates the following definitions of the extension of
\widetilde{\alpha}(P)
to the three-valued case
and of the CL-basic forms.
It will be seen that
({\sf F}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}b\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf T})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}({\sf U}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}b\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf U})
is the basic form according
to the ordering
a<b
, while this is
({\sf F}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf U})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}b\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}({\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf U})

if
b<a
.
Thus, the shared alphabet of
each
of the three terms in this example must be
\{a,b\}
.

Definition 4.2
.

For
P
a mem-basic form,
its
shared alphabet

\widetilde{\alpha}(P)

is the following set of atoms, defined with help of the auxiliary function

f:\textit{MBF}_{A}^{\,{\sf U}}\times 2^{A}\to 2^{A}
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\alpha}(P)=f(P,\alpha(P)),

\displaystyle f({\sf U},x)=x,

\displaystyle f({\sf T},x)=f({\sf F},x)=\emptyset,

\displaystyle f(P_{1}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}P_{2},x)=\{a\}\cup(f(P_{1},x\setminus\{a\})\cap f(P_{2},x\setminus\{a\})).

For each
\sigma\in A^{s}
, we write
F_{\sigma}(\vec{}{\sf U})
for

F_{\sigma}(P_{0},...,P_{2^{|\sigma|}-1})
if for all
i
,
P_{i}={\sf U}
(where
|\sigma|
determines the
length of
\vec{}{\sf U}
).

Lemma 4.3
.

If
P
is a mem-basic form with only
{\sf U}
-leaves, then (i)
\widetilde{\alpha}(P)=\alpha(P)
and
(ii) for all
\sigma\in A^{s}
,
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}^{\sf U}\vdash P=F_{\sigma}(\vec{}{\sf U})={\sf U}
.

Proof.

Both (i) and (ii) follow by structural induction on
P
.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

If
P
is a mem-basic form
and
a\in\widetilde{\alpha}(P)
, then

\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}^{\sf U}\vdash P=\ell_{a}(P)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}r_{a}(P).

Proof.

Like the proof of Lemma
3.5
.
∎

We note that the analogue of Lemma
3.6
is not valid:
if
P
and
Q
are mem-basic forms and

\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}^{\sf U}\vdash P=Q
, then
\widetilde{\alpha}(P)=\widetilde{\alpha}(Q)
does not necessarily hold, e.g
\widetilde{\alpha}({\sf U})=\emptyset
and

\widetilde{\alpha}({\sf U}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf U})=\{a\}
.

Definition 4.5
.

A mem-basic form
P
is a
CLU-basic form
if
for the unique
\sigma\in(\widetilde{\alpha}(P))^{s}
of length
|\widetilde{\alpha}(P)|

that agrees with
(A,<)
,
P=F_{\sigma}(P_{0},...,P_{2^{|\sigma|}-1})

and the following conditions are satisfied:

(i)

for some
j\in\{0,...,2^{|\sigma|}-1\}
,
P_{j}\neq{\sf U}
, and

(ii)

for all
i\in\{0,...,2^{|\sigma|}-1\}
,

P_{i}
is a CLU-basic form.

We will often write
CUbf
instead of “CLU-basic form”.
For example,
{\sf U}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf T}
is a CUbf.
Note that the above definition implies that
F_{\rho}(\vec{}{\sf U})
is not a CUbf for any

\rho\in A^{s}\setminus\{\epsilon\}
. More generally, a mem-basic form in
\textit{MBF}_{A}^{\,{\sf U}}
of depth larger than 0 with
only
{\sf U}
-leaves is not a CUbf.

Lemma 4.6
.

If
P
is a mem-basic form
and
\sigma\in(\widetilde{\alpha}(P))^{s}
has maximal length and agrees with
(A,<)
,
then there exist mem-basic forms
P_{0},...,P_{2^{|\sigma|}-1}
such that

\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}^{\sf U}\vdash P=F_{\sigma}(P_{0},...,P_{2^{|\sigma|}-1}).

Proof.

By induction on the depth of
P
. The base case
d(P)=0
is trivial.
If
d(P)>0
and

\sigma=a\rho
, then
\rho
represents the ordering of the shared alphabets of
the mem-basic forms
\ell_{a}(P)
and
r_{a}(P)
that
are both of lesser depth, hence

\displaystyle\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}^{\sf U}\vdash P

\displaystyle=\ell_{a}(P)\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}r_{a}(P)

(by Lemma
4.4
)

\displaystyle=F_{\rho}(P_{0},...,P_{2^{|\rho|}-1})\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}F_{\rho}(P_{0}^{\prime},...,P_{2^{|\rho|}-1}^{\prime})

(IH)

\displaystyle=F_{\sigma}(P_{0},...,P_{2^{|\sigma|}-1})

if
P_{2^{|\rho|}+i}=P_{i}^{\prime}
for
i=0,...,2^{|\rho|}-1
.
∎

Theorem 4.7
.

For each mem-basic form
P
there is a unique
CLU-basic form
Q
such that

\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}\vdash P=Q.

Proof.

By induction on the depth of
P
.
If
d(P)=0
then
P\in\{{\sf T},{\sf F},{\sf U}\}
and we are done.

If
d(P)>0
then
|\widetilde{\alpha}(P)|=n
for some
n\geq 1
.
For the unique
\sigma\in(\widetilde{\alpha}(P))^{s}
of length
n
that agrees with
(A,<)

and Lemma
4.6
,

\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}^{\sf U}\vdash P=F_{\sigma}(P_{0},...,P_{2^{n}-1})
.
By induction, there are unique CUbfs
Q_{i}

such that for all
i
,
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}^{\sf U}\vdash P_{i}=Q_{i}
.
The following two cases have to be distinguished:

1.

For all
i
,
Q_{i}={\sf U}
. Then
Q={\sf U}
is a CUbf,
and by Lemma
4.3
, it is the unique CUbf
that satisfies
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}^{\sf U}\vdash P=Q
.

2.

For some
j\in\{0,...,2^{|\sigma|}-1\}
,
Q_{j}\neq{\sf U}
.
Then
Q=F_{\sigma}(Q_{0},...,Q_{2^{|\sigma|}-1})
is a CUbf
and therefore it is the unique CUbf over
(\alpha(P),<)

that satisfies
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}^{\sf U}\vdash P=Q
.

∎

Example 4.8
.

Consider the mem-basic form
P
that we display graphically below on the left, and
observe that
\widetilde{\alpha}(P)=\{a,b,c\}
.
If
\textit{abc}\in A^{s}
agrees with
(A,<)
, the CLU-basic form of
P
is shown on the right.
