Note that the example remains valid
if in both
P
and its CUbf one of the leaves
{\sf T}
or
{\sf F}
is replaced by
{\sf U}
.
If another order of
\widetilde{\alpha}(P)
agrees with
(A,<)
, the corresponding CUbf
is easily found.
End example.

The mem-basic form function
\mathit{mbf}:{\mathcal{C}_{A}}\to{\mathcal{C}_{A}}
, defined in
[
4
]
and used in the previous section,
can be easily extended to
{\mathcal{C}_{A}^{\sf U}}\to{\mathcal{C}_{A}^{\sf U}}
; we show this in the Appendix.
This implies that for all
P\in{\mathcal{C}_{A}^{\sf U}}
,

\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{mem}}^{\sf U}\vdash P=\mathit{mbf}(P)
, by which we find the following result:

Corollary 4.9
.

For all
P,Q\in{\mathcal{C}_{A}^{\sf U}}
,
\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}^{\sf U}\vdash P=Q

if, and only if,

\llbracket P\rrbracket_{\textit{cl}}=\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\textit{cl}}
,
where
\llbracket P\rrbracket_{\textit{cl}}
is
P
’s unique CLU-basic form.

Corollary
4.9
and the above theorem imply that

\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}^{\sf U}
axiomatises three-valued
conditional valuation congruence, that is, the congruence on
{\mathcal{C}_{A}^{\sf U}}
defined by having equal CLU-basic forms.

It remains to be shown that
C
\ell
SCL
is completely axiomatised by the equational logic
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{3}
.

Theorem 4.10
.

For all terms
s,t
over
\Sigma_{\textup{$\textrm{SCL}$}}(A)
,
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{3}\vdash s=t~\iff~\textup{$\textrm{C$\ell$SCL}$}\vdash s=t
.

Proof.

As the proof of Theorem
3.13
, but now relying on Theorem
[
6
, Thm.7.16]
.
The extensions with
{\sf U}
in the corresponding intermediate results require
f({\sf U})={\sf U}
and
g({\sf U})={\sf U}
.
∎

Like in the previous section, we can use CLU-basic forms to define “normal forms” in the
equational theory of
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{3}
:
for
P\in{\mathcal{S}_{A}^{\sf U}}
we find
f(P)\in{\mathcal{C}_{A}^{\sf U}}
,
and thus, given an ordering
(A,<)
, a unique CLU-basic form
\mathit{mbf}(f(P))
such that

\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{cl}}^{\sf U}\vdash f(P)=\mathit{mbf}(f(P))
and thus

\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{3}\vdash P=g(f(P))=g(\mathit{mbf}(f(P))),

so
g(\mathit{mbf}(f(P)))
can be defined as the normal form of
P
.

5
Independent axiomatisations of CL

Guzmán and Squier state in
[
11
]
that the seven equational axioms in Table
1
,
i.e.

\displaystyle x^{\prime\prime}=x

(
1.1a
)

\displaystyle(x\wedge y)^{\prime}=x^{\prime}\vee y^{\prime}

(
1.1b
)

\displaystyle(x\wedge y)\wedge z=x\wedge(y\wedge z)

(
1.1c
)

\displaystyle x\wedge(y\vee z)=(x\wedge y)\vee(x\wedge z)

(
1.1d
)

\displaystyle(x\vee y)\wedge z=(x\wedge z)\vee(x^{\prime}\wedge y\wedge z)

(
1.1e
)

\displaystyle x\vee(x\wedge y)=x

(
1.1f
)

\displaystyle(x\wedge y)\vee(y\wedge x)=(y\wedge x)\vee(x\wedge y)

(
1.1g
)

are to the best of their knowledge independent. In this section we show that this is not the case
and provide several axiomatisations that are independent. Also, we prove that
\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}\prec\textup{$\textrm{C$\ell$SCL${}_{2}$}$}
.

As an alternative to axiom (
1.1e
) that does not assume the associative law (
1.1c
), we consider both possible
readings of (
1.1e
):

\displaystyle(x\vee y)\wedge z=(x\wedge z)\vee(x^{\prime}\wedge(y\wedge z)),\hskip 147.95433pt

(Mem1)

\displaystyle(x\vee y)\wedge z=(x\wedge z)\vee((x^{\prime}\wedge y)\wedge z).\hskip 147.95433pt

(Mem2)

Furthermore, we shall write
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}^{\sf U}
for
CL
with (only) the constant
{\sf U}
distinguished.
For the axiomatisations of
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}^{\sf U}
,
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}
and
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{3}
(thus,
{\sf U}
or/and
{\sf T}
and
{\sf F}
are distinguished),
the following axioms are provided in
[
11
]
:

\displaystyle{\sf U}^{\prime}={\sf U}

(1.4a)

\displaystyle{\sf T}\wedge x=x

(1.4b)

\displaystyle{\sf T}^{\prime}={\sf F}

(1.4c)

In the proofs below, we used the theorem prover
Prover9
and the tool

Mace4
for generating finite models, for both tools see
[
16
]
.
Derivations from equational axiomatisations were found with
Prover9
,
and
Mace4
was used to prove all independence results.
We used these tools on a Macbook Pro with a 2.4GHz
dual-core Intel Core i5 processor and 4GB of RAM.

.

(i)

or
(ii)

Proof.

It suffices to show that the axioms in (i) and (ii) follow from (

)–(
1.1g
)
with the appropriate reading of (

and that both axiomatisations of

(i)

pass

This follows with

, best result with options

<36^{\prime\prime}

Completeness of the axiomatisations in (i)–(v) follows from the completeness results

.

The two axiomatisations in (v) of
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{3}
are independent, this quickly follows with

.
This implies that all axiomatisations in (i)–(iv) are also independent.
∎

The

Below, we discuss an alternative axiomatisation based on these observations.

Theorem 5.2
.

(i)
Conditional logic with no constants distinguished
(
CL
)
is completely axiomatised by
the five axioms
\neg\neg x=x
and (
Or
), (
Abs
), (
Mem
), and (
Com
)
from Table
4
.
Moreover, these axioms are independent.

(ii)
Conditional logic with
{\sf T}
and
{\sf F}
distinguished
(
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}
)
is completely axiomatised by
the six axioms of
EqCL
(see Table
4
).
Moreover, these axioms are independent.

(iii)
Conditional logic with only
{\sf U}
distinguished
(
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}^{\sf U}
)
is completely axiomatised by
the six axioms
\neg\neg x=x
,
\neg{\sf U}={\sf U}
, and (
Or
), (
Abs
), (
Mem
) and (
Com
) in
Table
4
.
Moreover, these axioms are independent.

(iv)
Conditional logic with all constants distinguished
(
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{3}
)
is completely axiomatised by
the seven axioms of
\textup{$\textrm{EqCL}$}^{\sf U}
(see Table
4
).
Moreover, these axioms are independent.

Proof.

It follows easily (either by hand or with help of
Prover9
) that the axioms in each of (i)–(iv)
both imply those of the associated axiomatisation in
Theorem
5.1

that uses the axioms in
5.1
.(i),
and
are their consequences.
This proves the mentioned completeness results.

The independence of the axiomatisations (iii) and (iv) follows quickly with
Mace4
,
and implies the independence of (i) and (ii), respectively.
∎

Corollary 5.3
.

\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}\prec\textup{$\textrm{C$\ell$SCL${}_{2}$}$}
and
\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}^{\sf U}\prec\textup{$\textrm{C$\ell$SCL}$}
, i.e., for all terms
s,t
over
\Sigma_{\textup{$\textrm{SCL}$}}(A)
,

\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}\vdash s=t~\Longrightarrow~\textup{$\textrm{C$\ell$SCL${}_{2}$}$}\vdash s=t
, and for all terms
s,t
over
\Sigma_{\textup{$\textrm{SCL}$}}^{\sf U}(A)
,

\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}^{\sf U}\vdash s=t~\Longrightarrow~\textup{$\textrm{C$\ell$SCL}$}\vdash s=t.

Proof.

EqMSCL
axiomatises
MSCL
and by Theorem
5.2
.(ii), it is a proper subset of
the independent axiomatisation
EqCL
of
C
\ell
SCL
2
. Hence,
\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}\prec\textup{$\textrm{C$\ell$SCL${}_{2}$}$}
.

Similarly,
\textup{$\textrm{EqMSCL}$}^{\sf U}
axiomatises
\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}^{\sf U}
and is by Theorem
5.2
.(iv) a proper subset of
the independent axiomatisation
\textup{$\textrm{EqCL}$}^{\sf U}
of
C
\ell
SCL
. Hence,
\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}^{\sf U}\prec\textup{$\textrm{C$\ell$SCL}$}
.
∎

6
Discussion and conclusions

We studied conditional logic and provided for its variants with distinguished
constants
{\sf T}
and
{\sf F}
, and with or without
{\sf U}
, a detailed explanation of its ‘basic forms’,
and included these variants in our framework of short-circuit logics.
In this section we address two questions: which logic is defined by conditional logic when restricting to
the full left-sequential connectives and negation, and what does it mean to define SCLs
without the constants
{\sf T}
and
{\sf F}
. We end with some conclusions and remarks on related and future work.

Full left-sequential evaluation is commutative in CL – consequences.

Let
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{3}(~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}},~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}})
denote the extension of
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{3}
with the connectives
{~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}}
and
{~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}}

and their defining axioms

x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y=(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}y)~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x)\quad\text{ and }\quad x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y=\neg(\neg x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg y).

Then
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{3}(~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}},~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}})
incorporates Bochvar’s

three-valued strict logic

[
7
]
.
In
[

]
, this logic is denoted as

and
it is proved that

is completely axiomatised by the following axioms:
5
5

5

With
Prover9
it quickly follows that these axioms are consequences of
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{3}(~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}},~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}})
.
Moreover, in
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}(~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}},~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}})
and
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}(~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}},~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}})
, the corresponding version of the
axiomatisation of
\mathbb{S}_{3}
, i.e.

(S1), (S3), (S4), (S6)–(S9) and (S3), (S4), (S8)–(S10), respectively,

is also derivable (the latter set implies (S6)).
The two-valued variant of
\mathbb{S}_{3}
, say
\mathbb{S}_{2}
, satisfies

\mathbb{S}_{2}\prec\textup{ ``propositional logic with $\neg$, $~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}$ and $~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}$''}

because the absorption law
x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y)=x
and its commutative variants (and duals) do not hold
in
\mathbb{S}_{2}
(a counter-model is quickly found by
Mace4
).

Short-circuit logics without constants.

Obviously, all two-valued SCLs can be defined without the constants
{\sf T}
and
{\sf F}
.
In the case of
FSCL
, this significantly reduces its axiomatisation,
a complete set of axioms is
\neg\neg x=x
,
x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}y=\neg(\neg x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg y)
,
and
(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}y)~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}z=x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}z)
(as noted in
[
17
]
).
All other axioms of
FSCL
involve (sub)terms with only
{\sf F}
-leaves (or with only
{\sf T}
-leaves),
such as
x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F}=\neg x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F}
, and these cannot be expressed in
FSCL
without
{\sf T}
and
{\sf F}
.
Hence, the full left-sequential connectives are not definable in
FSCL
without
{\sf T}
and
{\sf F}
.

Let
\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}_{0}
be defined as
MSCL
, but without the constants
{\sf T}
and
{\sf F}
(which are not definable).
This is similar to the way CL is set up, leaving open the choice to adjoin
{\sf T}
and
{\sf F}
,
and
{\sf U}
.
From the axiomatisation of
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}
in Table
4
(Theorem
5.2
.(ii)),
a complete axiomatisation of
\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}_{0}
, say
\textup{$\textrm{EqMSCL}$}_{0}
, is obtained by omitting
the axiom (
Com
) from those listed in Theorem
5.2
.(i).
6
6

6

\textup{$\textrm{EqMSCL}$}\vdash s=t\Rightarrow\textup{$\textrm{EqMSCL}$}_{0}\vdash s=t
follows by induction on the length of derivations.

Obviously,
\textup{$\textrm{EqMSCL}$}_{0}
is independent because the original axiomatisation is.
Moreover, full left-sequential conjunction is definable in
\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}_{0}
by

x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y=(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg y))~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}y\quad\text{or}\quad x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y=(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}y)~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x).

We conclude that
\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}_{0}
compared to
MSCL
imposes no constraints other
than the undefinability of
{\sf T}
and
{\sf F}
.

Finally, we note that
\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}_{0}\vdash(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg x)~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x=x,~(\neg x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x)~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x=x
,
the equations that are the counterparts of the axiom
{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x=x
(
Tand
),
so the question is indeed what would
be missed when using
\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}_{0}
“in practice”.
We also note that omitting (
1.1g
) from each of the two axiomatisations of

\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}
in Theorem
5.1
does not yield a complete axiomatisation of
\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}_{0}
:

Mace4

finds a counter-model for
(\neg x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x)~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x=x
, and for

x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg x=\neg x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x
.

For the case of
\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}^{\sf U}_{0}
(with
{\sf U}
and the axiom
\neg{\sf U}={\sf U}
), similar observations apply.

Conclusions.

Starting from Guzmán and Squier’s conditional logic, we introduced the two-valued
short-circuit logic
C
\ell
SCL
2
,
axiomatised by
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}
, and three-valued
C
\ell
SCL
, axiomatised by
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{3}
. Then we analysed the commutativity
of the full left-sequential connectives in
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{3}
and found that these and negation
define Bochvar’s three-valued strict logic.
Finally, we investigated the independence of the equational axiomatisations of
CL
, and
paid attention to short-circuit logic without constants.

We conclude with two remarks.
1) Three equations that hold in
\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}_{0}
and express properties related to falsehood are

x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg x=\neg x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x
and
(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg x)~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x=x
and
(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg x)~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}y=x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg x
.
Another equation that relates to falsehood and does not hold in
MSCL
, but does hold in
CL
, is

(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg x)~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg y)=(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg y)~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg x).

Furthermore,
x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg x=y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg y
is
not
a consequence of
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}
(by
Mace4
).
These facts support our conjecture that there exists no short-circuit logic without additional constants
in between
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}
and
SSCL
(i.e., the SCL that represents propositional logic in the signature
\{{~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}},{~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}},\neg,{\sf T},{\sf F}\}
).
We note that adding the axiom
x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg x=y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg y
to
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}
(or
(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg x)~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}y=y
,
or
x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F}={\sf F}
) yields
SSCL
(by
Prover9
). Of course, with
a\in A

and
|A|>1
, the ad-hoc SCL defined by
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}\cup\{(a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg a)~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}x=x\}
is in between
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{2}
and
SSCL
.

2) The equation
(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf U})~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf U}={\sf U}
, which follows immediately from
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}^{\sf U}
and
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{3}
,
can be seen as a concise characterisation of the difference between
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}^{\sf U}
and
\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}^{\sf U}_{0}
,
and of the difference between
\textup{$\textrm{CL}$}_{3}
and
\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}^{\sf U}
.

Related work.

In
[
18
]
, Staudt introduced evaluation trees and thereby
axiomatised
Free Fully Evaluated Logic
(FFEL), a sublogic of
FSCL
that has (only)
full left-sequential connectives (and, like
FSCL
, is immune to side effects):

\displaystyle{\sf F}

\displaystyle=\neg{\sf T}

(FEL1)

\displaystyle x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y

\displaystyle=\neg(\neg x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg y)

(FEL2)

\displaystyle\neg\neg x

\displaystyle=x

(FEL3)

\displaystyle(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y)~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}z

\displaystyle=x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}z)

(FEL4)

\displaystyle{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}x

\displaystyle=x

(FEL5)

\displaystyle x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf T}

\displaystyle=x

(FEL6)

\displaystyle x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F}

\displaystyle={\sf F}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}x

(FEL7)

\displaystyle x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F}

\displaystyle=\neg x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F}

(FEL8)

\displaystyle(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F})~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y

\displaystyle=(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf T})~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y

(FEL9)

\displaystyle x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F})

\displaystyle=x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf T})

(FEL10)

These axioms are consequences of
\mathbb{S}_{2}
(according to Prover9), but FFEL is obviously weaker:

x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y=y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}x
does not hold.
(Note: a completeness result for FSCL was proved in
[
18
]
.)

Future work.

In
[
6
]
, we mentioned the following attractive challenge:

Find
a convincing example that distinguishes
\textup{$\textrm{MSCL}$}^{\sf U}
from Conditional Logic as
defined in Guzmán and Squier (1990).

Our current research on “fracterm calculus for partial meadows” may well prove to be such a convincing example.
A partial meadow is a field with a partial division function for which division by 0 is undefined.
Fracterm calculus for partial meadows aims to formalise elementary arithmetic including division by defining a
three-valued sequential first-order logic, on top of a three-valued short-circuit logic with sequential,
noncommutative connectives. In the Tarski semantics for this first-order logic we have in mind,
conditional logic is sound and thus seems the right candidate to start from.

Appendix: A normalisation function for mem-basic forms

The two normalisation functions
\mathit{bf}
() for two-valued basic forms and
\mathit{mbf}
() for mem-basic forms, both defined in
[
4
]
,
can be easily lifted to the tree-valued setting.

Define the basic form function
\mathit{bf}:{\mathcal{C}_{A}^{\sf U}}\to\textit{BF}_{A}^{\>{\sf U}}
and, given
Q,R\in\textit{BF}_{A}^{\>{\sf U}}
,
the auxiliary function
[{\sf T}\mapsto Q,{\sf F}\mapsto R]:\textit{BF}_{A}^{\>{\sf U}}\to\textit{BF}_{A}^{\>{\sf U}}

for which postfix notation
P[{\sf T}\mapsto Q,{\sf F}\mapsto R]
is used, as follows:

\mathit{bf}(B)=B
for
B\in\{{\sf T},{\sf F},{\sf U}\}
,

\displaystyle{\sf T}[{\sf T}\mapsto Q,{\sf F}\mapsto R]=Q,

\mathit{bf}(a)={\sf T}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}{\sf F}
for
a\in A
,

\displaystyle{\sf F}[{\sf T}\mapsto Q,{\sf F}\mapsto R]=R,

\displaystyle\mathit{bf}(P\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}Q\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}R)=\mathit{bf}(Q)[{\sf T}\mapsto\mathit{bf}(P),{\sf F}\mapsto\mathit{bf}(R)],

\displaystyle{\sf U}[{\sf T}\mapsto Q,{\sf F}\mapsto R]={\sf U},

and
(P_{1}\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}P_{2})[{\sf T}\mapsto Q,{\sf F}\mapsto R]=P_{1}[{\sf T}\mapsto Q,{\sf F}\mapsto R]\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}P_{2}[{\sf T}\mapsto Q,{\sf F}\mapsto R]
.

From
[
6
, App.A8]
, it follows that for all
P\in{\mathcal{C}_{A}^{\sf U}}
,

\textup{$\textrm{CP}$}^{\sf U}\vdash P=\mathit{bf}(P)
,
and for all
P,Q\in{\mathcal{C}_{A}^{\sf U}}
,
\textup{$\textrm{CP}$}^{\sf U}\vdash P=Q
if, and only if,
\mathit{bf}(P)=\mathit{bf}(Q)
.

Next, define the mem-basic form function
\mathit{mbf}:{\mathcal{C}_{A}^{\sf U}}\to{\mathcal{C}_{A}^{\sf U}}
by
\mathit{mbf}(P)=\mathit{mf}(\mathit{bf}(P))
,
and the auxiliary function

\mathit{mf}:\textit{BF}_{A}^{\>{\sf U}}\to\textit{BF}_{A}^{\>{\sf U}}
by

\mathit{mf}(B)=B
for
B\in\{{\sf T},{\sf F},{\sf U}\}

and

\mathit{mf}(P\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}Q)=\mathit{mf}(\ell_{a}(P))\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\lhd\;$}}a\scalebox{0.78}{\raisebox{0.1pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\;\rhd\;$}}\mathit{mf}(r_{a}(Q)),

with
\ell_{a}()
and
r_{a}()
as in Definition
2.4
.

It follows easily that
\mathit{mbf}()
is a normalisation function yielding mem-basic forms:
the extension to
{\mathcal{C}_{A}^{\sf U}}
of Theorem 5.9 in
[
4
]
, i.e.

For all
P\in{\mathcal{C}_{A}^{\sf U}},\textup{$\textrm{{$\textrm{CP}$}}$}_{\textit{mem}}\vdash P=\mathit{mbf}(P)
,

and of the supporting lemmas is trivial: all inductive proofs in
[
4
]

require one additional, trivial base case (for
{\sf U}
).
